TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Some of Silicon Valley’s most influential executives, from SpaceX CEO Elon Musk to OpenAI co-founder Greg Brockman, collectively spent more than half a billion dollars backing Republican candidates. However, that didn’t appear to be enough to stop the U.S. midterms’ AI reckoning, FP’s Rishi Iyengar and Christina Lu wrote . Notably, several historically red states that host a vast number of data centers, such as Texas, swung blue. This has made investors worried. On Thursday, the Nasdaq dropped by 5.52 percent (or roughly $2.5 trillion ). The S&P 500 also fell by 4.36 percent, and the Dow Jones declined by 3.44 percent. All three index averages recorded their worst days since April 2025, when U.S. President Donald Trump launched his sweeping global trade war. * The U.S. Senate Just Got a Lot Younger
  Foreign Policy | 06 Nov 20:46 UTC
  The midterms put a huge dent in America’s gerontocracy.
  FULL TEXT: The U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday saw the biggest Senate turnover in nearly 50 years, with at least 15 new lawmakers elected to join the upper chamber come January. Several of Capitol Hill’s octogenarians will be leaving the Beltway, and a whole batch of younger legislators will be moving in. The United States once had one of the oldest average upper houses in the developed world, according to data from IPU Parline . As of this month, the average age of U.S. senators is 65.4 years old. Among members of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) for which data is available, the next eldest nation’s class is roughly five years younger, with Poland’s Senate having an average age of 61.9 years and the Netherlands’s Senate an average age of 58.4 years. The U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday saw the biggest Senate turnover in nearly 50 years, with at least 15 new lawmakers elected to join the upper chamber come January. Several of Capitol Hill’s octogenarians will be leaving the Beltway, and a whole batch of younger legislators will be moving in. The United States once had one of the oldest average upper houses in the developed world, according to data from IPU Parline . As of this month, the average age of U.S. senators is 65.4 years old. Among members of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) for which data is available, the next eldest nation’s class is roughly five years younger, with Poland’s Senate having an average age of 61.9 years and the Netherlands’s Senate an average age of 58.4 years. But Washington’s new crop of senators will reduce that number by quite a significant margin. The average age of the 15 departing U.S. senators is 70 years old. Their replacements are coming in at roughly age 55. That will bring the average age of the U.S. Senate down to at least 62.8 years—still above most of its OECD counterparts but a dramatic shift nonetheless. That also means that the Senate is moving toward closing the age gap between its members and the constituents they represent. According to the Economist , the United States had the least age-representative government within the OECD as of December 2024, with lawmakers more than 20 years older than the average population age. The outgoing class of senators has only eight members under age 50. The new class, however, will nearly double that number, with at least 15 lawmakers under 50. Although the eldest age will remain at 93 (Republican Sen. Chuck Grassley of 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/06/us-midterm-elections-senate-ages-average-decline-oecd/

* A German Movie Previews Its Far-Right Future
  Foreign Policy | 06 Nov 20:00 UTC
  The country’s cinema has recently taken a very dark turn.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Contemporary cinema from Germany seems determined to disabuse anyone—including Germans—who still imagines it as a nation of Birkenstock-wearing, anti-nuclear peaceniks who shrink from their own shadows. A spate of new, sharply edged films deftly take up where the socially critical New German Cinema of the 1970s—featuring directors such as Rainer Werner Fassbinder, Volker Schlöndorff, and Margarethe von Trotta—left off. This new Germany is something much darker than that portrayed in lighthearted films from recent decades like Fack ju Göhte and Good Bye, Lenin! , and the far right’s stunning results in September’s regional elections portend how much darker it may still become. One of these unflinching filmmakers is the German Iranian director Faraz Shariat, whose Prosecution is a harrowing thriller about a young German lawyer with Korean roots—a lesbian named Seyo Kim (Chen Emilie Yan)—who starts as a state prosecutor in a small eastern German city. Louise Kennedy (Riverhead Books, 288 pp., $28, November 2026) This month, we’re reading two new novels that explore the long shadow of shame and secrecy across Ireland and South Africa. Louise Kennedy (Riverhead Books, 288 pp., $28, November 2026) The first half of Stations , Louise Kennedy’s sophomore novel, reads like a typical tale of unrequited love—albeit with trademark Irish melancholy. But then things get properly dark. Kennedy tells the story in snippets that span decades. It begins in County Kildare, Ireland, in 1982, during the Troubles . Roisin McCaffrey—an “actively unpopular” teenager—strikes up an unlikely friendship with her rabble-rousing classmate, Redmond “Red” Camelin, whom she describes as “like Dennis the Menace.” Both have strained relationships with their fathers. Roisin’s father is an alcoholic who is unable to hold down a job. Although the McCaffreys are Northern Irish Catholics, he spent a year in the Royal Ulster Constabulary before moving the family south because he had become “a legitimate target for Republican paramilitaries.” Eventually, he leaves Roisin and her mother, settling in London with a new family that he also later abandons. Roisin “hated that people were known by what their fathers did, aware that she was known for her dad’s abscondment.” Less is immediately clear about Red’s father, though Red runs away from home while in secondary school, preferring to crash on friends’ couches than cohabitate with his parents. Roisin intuits that “there was something dreadful in Red’s family.” Roisin and Red engage in standard teenage flirtation and friendship—and, on one occasion, share a kiss—but Red insists that their relationship is strictly platonic. That’s not for Roisin’s lack of trying. After secondary school, she drops out of college to move to London, crashing in Red’s Pimlico squat and working odd jobs. Roisin amasses a roster of romantic conquests but never manages to interest Red. He, meanwhile, is drawn deeper into a dangerous drug scene. In the late 1980s—when “Hep C and HIV were rampant”—Roisin makes the difficult decision to cut ties with Red. Kennedy leaves a gap between 1989 and 2007, when she finds Roisin again, this time at her father’s funeral. * Who Will Succeed Xi Jinping?
  Foreign Policy | 06 Nov 16:18 UTC
  Provincial reshuffles may offer some clues.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Ever since Xi Jinping bucked norms and made it clear he would not be held to a two-term limit as president of China and general secretary of the Chinese Communist Party (CCP), there has been intense interest in who could succeed him. Xi has purged several possible successors, but he is also aging—and even if he tries to hold onto power, he may find it easier to appoint a chosen favorite. Although there is still no broadly recognized successor—and no such figure is likely to emerge before the CCP’s 21st National Congress late next year, if then—the intensive reshuffling of provincial officials on the eve of the Fifth Plenum may offer some clues. Ever since Xi Jinping bucked norms and made it clear he would not be held to a two-term limit as president of China and general secretary of the Chinese Communist Party (CCP), there has been intense interest in who could succeed him. Xi has purged several possible successors, but he is also aging—and even if he tries to hold onto power, he may find it easier to appoint a chosen favorite. Although there is still no broadly recognized successor—and no such figure is likely to emerge before the CCP’s 21st National Congress late next year, if then—the intensive reshuffling of provincial officials on the eve of the Fifth Plenum may offer some clues. On Sept. 4, Zhu Zhongming, born in 1972, was promoted from deputy party secretary of Shanghai to acting mayor, making him head of the government of China’s largest city. For a time, many observers took the move as evidence that Beijing was deliberately grooming Zhu as Xi’s future successor. But matters are not so simple. Provincial personnel changes have since accelerated. On Sept. 23, Anhui, Henan, and Fujian all got new party secretaries. Li Lecheng and Zhou Zuyi, both born in 1965, took over Anhui and Henan, respectively, while Zhao Long, born in 1967, was promoted from governor of Fujian to provincial party secretary. (In China’s system, the party secretary is a more important figure than the governor.) Then, on Oct. 24, two days before the Fifth Plenum opened, the pace quickened further. Wang Zhijun, born in 1965, became party secretary of Hunan, while Mo Gaoyi, born in 1964, took over in Yunnan. At the same time, younger officials moved into position for governorships: Guo Ningning, born in 1970, in Fujian; Zhuge Yujie, born in 1971, in Qinghai; and Xie Weijiang, born in 1973, in Hunan. This reveals a clear generational shift. 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/06/china-xi-jinping-successor-party-congress/

* ‘Simply staggering’: Rapidly developing ‘super El Niño’ on track to peak in December
  Carbon Brief | 06 Nov 09:12 UTC
  The ongoing “super El Niño” is likely to peak at the end of 2026 and push average global temperatures to… The post ‘Simply staggering’: Rapidly developing ‘super El Niño’ on track to peak in December appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The ongoing “super El Niño” is likely to peak at the end of 2026 and push average global temperatures to record levels next year. That is according to Carbon Brief’s latest “ state of the climate ” update, which analyses El Niño forecasts from more than a dozen climate models, as well as six global temperature datasets. Dr Zeke Hausfather , Carbon Brief’s climate science contributor, described this year’s El Niño as “simply staggering” when answering questions from CB+ members during a CB Connect “ ask me anything ” session. El Niño is a naturally occurring climate pattern in the tropical Pacific that releases heat from the ocean into the atmosphere and temporarily boosts global temperatures. This year’s El Niño event – which has been underway since June – has already broken a number of records and reshaped the world’s weather, fuelling fires in Indonesia and “super typhoons” in the west Pacific. The strength of an El Niño is tracked using the “Niño3.4 anomaly”, which is a measure of how much warmer sea surface temperatures are than average in a section of the central Pacific. Analysis of more than 600 forecasts from 14 different climate models by Hausfather for Carbon Brief’s “state of the climate” update shows how models project this year’s El Niño will peak with an anomaly of around 4C. This would be more than a degree above the previous record in 2015-16. Writing on CB Connect on 5 November, Hausfather wrote: “This El Niño event is developing so quickly and is reaching sea surface temperatures so warm that it’s simply staggering. We haven’t seen anything like this in the historical record and it may end up being the strongest El Niño event in the past millennium.” This year’s El Niño has already chalked up a number of milestones. On 18 September, the daily anomaly in the Niño3.4 region broke the satellite-era record set in 2015 when it reached 3.11C. Meanwhile, September as a whole recorded the highest monthly Niño3.4 region temperature anomaly – 2.84C – since sea surface temperature records began in the mid-1800s. Hausfather wrote on CB Connect that this year’s El Niño had been “running well above any prior El Niño event since June, at times by a full degree [Celsius]”. Carbon Brief’s “state of the climate” analysis indicates that El Niño is set to continue to intensify into December, with 10 out of 14 models expecting temperatures in the Niño3.4 region to peak in the final month of this year. The remainder anticipate a November peak. While human emis
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/simply-staggering-rapidly-developing-super-el-nino-on-track-to-peak-in-december

* The Art of Letting Go
  Longreads | 06 Nov 13:00 UTC
  "On the poetics of loss."
  FULL TEXT: For The American Scholar , Lisa Russ Spaar offers us beautiful litany of loss in all its various shapes and forms. From those we love to objects like keys, from our teeth to our moral compass, Spaar takes a close look at what we go through when we experience loss at various times in our lives, and how losses tend to stack up as we age. I’ve informally polled several friends, colleagues, and family members about their first remembered losses. For most, it was when a milk tooth fell out, usually around the age of six. (Surely there are primal reasons many of us dream throughout our lifetimes about our teeth, as important as they are to speaking, eating.) More than 20 years ago, one of my talented undergraduates, Molly, wrote a poem about losing her first tooth while sledding in winter. Upon tonguing the little bloody socket that remained, she wrote, “I taste my death.” That line has haunted me over the decades. Again, our bodies: made for loss. Language: our ability to articulate what that might mean. I often wonder what was happening in my late mother’s mind as she sank, over a period of 10-plus years, into the tangled ambages of dementia. She lost the names of things and people. She lost any memory of who we were, those of us who cared for her, even her devoted husband, my father. She finally even forgot that she was not only an alcoholic with a fifth of Scotch hidden in the breadbox but also a chain smoker, though she’d occasionally hold a drinking straw to her lips and take a long, deep drag. “In the world of jigsaws, there can be a fine line between productivity and pleasure.” “What Oliver Sacks jotted down in the books he read.” “On guns, MTV, Stephen King, and the nightmare from which we cannot awake.”

* The Poppy’s Many Meanings
  New Lines | 06 Nov 11:42 UTC
  The flower is used to commemorate sacrifice in war, but the humble weed has far more negative connotations in some countries. The post The Poppy’s Many Meanings appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: A planted seed is generally left well alone to sprout and grow in the darkness, perhaps with the help of water or fertilizer. But there is a notable exception: Poppies require light to germinate, meaning the earth must be disturbed before they grow. This feature of the flower explains much about its ubiquity and patterns across landscapes. It appears in particular density in places where earth has been turned over, such as farmland or cemeteries — or where trenches have been dug to protect troops in war. Nov. 11 is Remembrance Day across the Commonwealth, with poppies seen on lapels throughout the month, and memorial events held on the Sunday closest to the day itself — this year, this coming Sunday. These spots of color on coats symbolize remembrance of, and gratitude to, those who lost their lives fighting in war, drawing on images from World War I of the poppies that sprouted, undeterred by violence, all along the front lines of Europe. These front lines shifted regularly, often by tiny amounts, but each time requiring more trenches to be dug and more soil to be disrupted — so that more poppies grew. Poppies on battlefields had been noted during the Napoleonic Wars of the early 19th century, but the image was cemented in the minds of generations of schoolchildren by a poem scribbled in the back of an ambulance during World War I, by Canadian physician John McCrae. It begins: “In Flanders fields, the poppies blow / Between the crosses, row on row / That mark our place.” Its patriotism, signaling the rightness of dying for one’s country, is suitable for the military parades of Remembrance Sunday, and the simplicity and the color of the poppy make it an ideal emblem, adopted in 1921 by the newly formed Royal British Legion to fundraise to help those who served in the war. Neither the poem nor the poppy are uncontroversial, however. “Take up our quarrel with the foe!” the last verse begins, signaling a commitment to conflict many find abhorrent; these people shun the poppy or instead choose to wear a white version, symbolizing peace rather than war. But there are far wider problems, worldwide, with what poppies represent. From the opium wars in China to the poppy fields of India and Afghanistan to the opioid crisis in America, the poppy has been a source of addiction, conflict and misery — and, for a few, wealth. And these dichotomies, between wealth and grinding poverty, memories of noble sacrifice or of oppression and addiction, are not the only meanings 
  https://newlinesmag.com/essays/the-poppys-many-meanings/

* Celestial nuisance
  Aeon | 06 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The cosmos has become a lab for the Big Questions in physics. Along the way, we’ve lost sight of its glorious messiness - by Dylan Jow Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Journey to a satellite galaxy roughly 200,000 light-years from Earth, featuring the young star cluster NGC 602. With data from Webb’s NIRCam ( Near-InfraRed Camera ) and MIRI ( Mid-InfraRed Instrument ). Courtesy HUBBLE/WEBB/ESA In the story of cosmic history, one of the most important supporting characters is dust. As clouds of interstellar gas cooled and condensed in the early Universe, dust grains absorbed and re-emitted radiation, eventually forming stars. Later, dust provided the raw material from which planets could form and grow. Dust grains may even have been the surfaces on which the first complex organic molecules formed. Yet, if you ask most cosmologists, they’ll tell you that dust is a sheer nuisance. In astrophysics, ‘nuisance’ is a quasi-technical term. Astrophysicists call any phenomenon that gets in the way of the thing they actually want to study a ‘nuisance’. Dust grains emit polarised light that can mimic subtle signals from the afterglow of the Big Bang (the cosmic microwave background) that cosmologists have spent decades and billions of dollars trying to detect. A cosmologist studies dust not to uncover its role in shaping the cosmos, but to subtract it – the term ‘sweeping under the rug’ couldn’t be more apt. This dichotomy between ‘signal’ and ‘nuisance’ is found across many sub-fields in astronomy. Astronomers who study a phenomenon called ‘weak lensing’ use the subtle distortions of light caused by gravity to map invisible dark matter. For them, the ordinary matter that makes up everything we can see is a nuisance. Pulsar timing astronomers use rapidly spinning neutron stars called pulsars as extraordinarily precise clocks to detect the passage of gravitational waves through the galaxy. For them, the nuisance is ionised interstellar gas, which scatters and disperses the light from pulsars. Yet one astronomer’s nuisance is another’s treasure. Just as a laser beam reveals the intricate swirls of smoke in a dark room, pulsars illuminate the fine structure of the interstellar medium, the unlit space between galaxies. If you want to study the interstellar medium and its role in shaping our galaxy’s evolution, the light-scattering gas is an invaluable resource. The ESO-operated Very Large Telescope based at the Cerro Paranal site in the Atacama Desert of northern Chile. Photo by Miguel Claro/ESO Whether a phenomenon is a nuisance or something worth studying in its own right clearly depends on the investigator and their interests. But in
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-astrophysics-needs-to-abandon-the-big-questions

  "Feral parakeets flock to London."
  FULL TEXT: London’s feral parakeets are an invasive species—but they are also beautiful, and David Wingrave takes the chance to linger on their nightly roost at Hither Green Cemetery before asking whether they should be here at all. Along the way, he asks deeper questions: how we decide where an animal “belongs,” when (if ever) it becomes native, and why we are so uneasy about seeing bright green birds in London’s parks. The fix O’Gorman endorses may sound drastic, but his precautionary principle is shared by invasion biology, the subdiscipline devoted to the study of species transplanted from their native ranges. There is a long history of organisms moved by humans, whether deliberately or inadvertently, wreaking total havoc upon their new homes. The brown tree snake, native to Indonesia, northern Australia, and Papua New Guinea, was accidentally introduced to Guam by the US military following World War II and promptly ate its way through ten of the island’s twelve endemic forest bird species. Similarly, Nile perch released into Lake Victoria by British colonial forces gulped down roughly two hundred species of homegrown cichlids, fish found nowhere else on Earth. Cane toads in Australia, Miconia trees on Hawaii and Tahiti, rats just about everywhere—the list goes on. And if biodiversity loss, however regrettable, seems like an abstraction in the scheme of things, know that human culture frequently suffers direct impoverishments because of invasives. Chestnut blight, discovered in the United States in 1904 after chestnut trees were imported from Japan, effectively deleted the American chestnut tree from the continent; its products were central to the diet and lifestyles of various indigenous peoples and communities across Appalachia. Phylloxera, an aphid relative, was imported to Europe on American grapevines during the second half of the nineteenth century. The subsequent infestation resulted in the destruction, by some estimates, of 90 percent of all European vineyards. “Godwits make the longest non-stop migration in the bird world. That might be the least of their marvels. Can they survive a changing planet?” “A couple of years after I began volunteering, I realized I was hell-bent on healing birds because they, too, had healed me—in more ways than one.” “Millions of songbirds in Southeast Asia are trappe
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/05/the-parakeets-london-invasive-species/

The decision reflects a commitment to protect the kingdom, but it could also drag Egypt into a regional conflict that continues to escalate. While Egypt clearly views Saudi security as intricately tied to regional stability, any military maneuver risks provoking a sustained retaliation. Strikes targeting Egyptian infrastructure could weaken its already fragile economy, resulting in even more domestic and regional unrest. That partly explains Egypt’s traditionally cautious foreign policy, which has continued under President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi. In 2015, Egypt did not partake in the Saudi-led coalition against the Houthis, nor did it join the recent Mecca Pact — a defense alliance between Turkey, Saudi Arabia and Pakistan. Egypt now believes that staying on the sidelines threatens its security more than asserting its might. The Houthis are already trying to change Egypt’s assessment. On Oct. 27, the Iran-backed group stated that they consider Cairo’s decision to aid Riyadh a “declaration of war.” The statement came a day after Egypt’s Parliament approved Sisi’s request to provide military support to Saudi Arabia if called upon to do so. Ahmed Aboudouh, an associate fellow of the Middle East and North Africa Program at Chatham House, believes that Egypt is preparing to proactively manage the risks. He told New Lines that the operations were likely to be defensive, but that this could mean not only defending from within Saudi territory, but also defending Egypt by attempting to limit the Houthis’ ability to attack Egyptian targets. “My sense is that Egypt’s role will be confined to air and naval defense,” Aboudouh added. He explained that Saudi Arabia’s vast size and critical assets make it difficult to intercept the barrage of Houthi airstrikes — a challenge that Egypt could help with. On Oct. 10, the Houthis hit Riyadh International Airport , killing 13 people and injuring more than 300. It was the deadliest attack on the kingdom in more than a decade. In addition, Aboudouh expects Egyptian naval vessels to enter the Bab al-Mandab Strait to protect the strategic route from Houthi attacks. Commercial ships traverse this critical strait to reach the Suez Canal, a lifeline for Egypt’s economy and one of its most reliable sources of foreign currency. They come to think of migrants and asylum seekers as potential gangsters, rapists, or petty criminals, whose asylum claims are almost always suspect, their stories exaggerated to take advantage of the freedoms and generosity of the state. It’s one thing to theorize about this dehumanization but something else to hear it plainly acknowledged by the people enacting it. One former Latino colleague who still works as an agent told me that, at the height of COVID, “we were deporting everybody, and there was this mother who coughed on me in front of her kids, and I was like, ‘Don’t fucking cough on me!’ ” He shook his head recalling the encounter. “It was like a seesaw battle—I’m yelling, then I’m apologizing, then I’m giving all these people food, then I’m saying, ‘Shut the fuck up.’ ” After a pause, he continued, “You see these people like cattle. So I remember I went home and I cried. You don’t want to treat humans like that.” “In Southern California’s Otay Mountain Wilderness, the federal surveillance network seems unavoidable.   FULL TEXT: Longreads has published hundreds of original stories—personal essays, reported features, reading lists, and more—and more than 14,000 editor’s picks. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. Ashley Stimpson | Longreads | November 5, 2026 | 18 minutes (4,985 words) In the months leading up to the nation’s bicentennial, the rickety wooden wheels of covered wagons began to roll in every corner of the United States—from the evergreen forests of Washington and windswept prairies of North Dakota to the granite peaks of Maine and palm tree-lined suburbs of Central Florida. Over the next year, the Bicentennial Wagon Train (BWT) would commemorate the founding of the country by performing the reverse of Westward Expansion, as five caravans—eventually made up of as many as 300 horse-drawn wagons—snaked their way toward Valley Forge, where they would arrive on July 4, 1976, just in time to celebrate America’s 200th birthday. Along the way, the wagon trains would stop in hundreds of cities and towns, where residents could watch teams of wagoneers construct historically accurate encampments and perform a musical revue. If so moved, citizens could also sign a so-called rededication scroll, to recommit themselves to building a more perfect union. “To commemorate this nation’s Bicentennial, we hereby dedicate ourselves anew to the precepts of our Founding Fathers,” each signer promised, inking their name on a sheath of scratchy parchment paper. Large community dinners and solemn ceremonies were planned around these vows to democracy; in some cities, whole elementary schools and entire churches lined up to sign. To accommodate those not on the wagons’ path, scroll-signing centers were set up in Holiday Inns from coast to coast. The scrolls were then loaded onto the Mayflower moving van that accompanied each train and carried toward Pennsylvania, where organizers planned to have them microfilmed and placed in an aluminum time capsule interred in the Pennsylvania dirt for the next 100 years. In 2076, organizers of the Bicentennial Wagon Train imagined, future Americans would unearth these scrolls and feel a deep sense of appreciation and admiration for the Americans of the past. Altogether, 22 million people (a tenth of the population at the time) signed approximately 917,000 scrolls, a stack of paper as high as a 20-story building . By all accounts these scrolls really did end up in Pennsylvania, where President Gerald Ford signed one, right before

The post How MIT Trained the Shah’s Nuclear Engineers appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: On June 6, 1974, Jerome Wiesner booked into room 1537 at the Royal Tehran Hilton in preparation for his audience with the Shah of Iran. The electrical engineer had just celebrated his 59th birthday. In his six decades, he’d been chief scientific adviser to JFK, a name on Richard Nixon’s list of political enemies, and was now the 13th president of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT). While his friends and family had mulled over what to gift him on his birthday, Wiesner was occupied by his own deliberations over what to gift the shah. “How about one of the crystals?” a colleague posited in a letter, referring to those grown aboard NASA’s first space station. “Two of them are said to be the most perfect crystals ever grown – because of the zero g environment, of course.” The MIT professors in possession of the perfect crystals might not be willing to part with an entire specimen, he admitted. “But I should think you might be able to persuade them to donate at least a millimeter slice.” Convinced, Wiesner arranged for a sliver of the space gem to be mounted on a velvet cushion. He was already in Tehran when an angry NASA official called MIT and mumbled about the upset at the Space Center. MIT was informed that “upper levels of NASA have ‘hit the ceiling.’” Turned out it wasn’t clear who actually owned the crystals, and Wiesner’s history as a thorn in NASA’s side didn’t help matters much. Mohammad Reza Pahlavi never received the perfect space crystal (gifted instead to President Gerald Ford a few months later), but Wiesner gave him something else at that meeting in Tehran that would end up being far more useful and far more polarizing. In 1975, MIT agreed to train two cohorts of 27 Iranian nuclear scientists in a three-year graduate program that would provide them with the skills necessary to run the reactors in Iran’s fledgling nuclear industry. The students were handpicked by the shah’s new atomic energy chief and had all expenses paid. At MIT, the special program caused an uproar that eventually receded into history, packed into numbered boxes in the university’s archives. That’s where I found them: the letters, speeches, reports from student journalists, transcripts from heated faculty meetings, notes from impassioned student activists, scribbled memos from secretaries — records of a debate that underscored a fundamental question: Who “deserves” technology? * A View From Ohio on Election Day
  New Lines | 03 Nov 17:25 UTC
  To many Americans, the Buckeye State, like its miles of flatlands, is an archetype of the middle ground, a kind of baseline for the ordinary. But as the midterm election frenzy comes to an end, the state’s outsize role offers enduring lessons for a country in flux. The post A View From Ohio on Election Day appeared first on New Lines Maga...
  FULL TEXT: Mention “Ohio” to an American teen, and you might get a grimace or a knowing grin. In the last decade or so, the state’s name has become internet slang for what Merriam-Webster defines as “awkward, cringeworthy, or otherwise undesirable.” But long before it was a meme, Ohio held an unflattering place in the American imagination. To much of the country, the Buckeye State, like its miles of flatlands, is an archetype of the middle ground, a kind of baseline for the ordinary. The contemporary American folk singer John Gorka described his home state of New Jersey, the Big Apple’s less glamorous neighbor, as “like Ohio, but even more so.” Here, along the Midwest’s fields and byways, the best summers taste of frozen custard and Lake Erie perch, of sweet corn that is “knee-high by the Fourth of July.” Now, the warm days are waning, and a popular corn maze cuts through the bare stalks in the nearby Cuyahoga Valley. Soon to be behind us, too, is this year’s midterm election frenzy, during which Democrats and Republicans have scrambled to win over the country’s mythical median voter. And where better to stage that fight than in Ohio? Every couple of years, it seems, the state’s reputation as a kind of blank canvas lends it outsize political heft: Pundits and politicians throw various shades of red and blue at its citizenry, who, as anyone who’s lived here will tell you, neither seek nor particularly care for the attention. Ohio’s on-again, off-again popularity with the rest of the country makes sense, I suppose. Once a reliably “purple” place — just as likely to be among the electoral map’s red Republican states as its blue Democratic ones — it has seemed, over the last three presidential election cycles, to have swung decidedly right. But despite Donald Trump and his allies winning handily each time they have run here, his picks this year seem to be floundering. * Believing and thinking
  Aeon | 03 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The separation of church and state has an unspoken psychological basis: we choose what we believe, but not what we think - by Neil Van Leeuwen & Jacob Eisler Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Fryeburg, Maine, US. Photo by Andrew Lichtenstein/Corbis/Getty Images The idea of separating church and state – now entrenched in many modern liberal democracies – is in some ways strange. After all, governments must keep themselves informed and inform their citizens. That’s why they turn to the science of meteorology to predict weather patterns, to economics to understand patterns of growth and decline, and to the medical sciences to improve public health. So why wouldn’t governments also keep close alliances with religious institutions to know what’s going on with God? Separating church and state appears strange from other perspectives as well. In the historical sweep of large-scale human societies, it’s a relative newcomer, often associated with the United States’ founding father Thomas Jefferson. In 1802, Jefferson asserted the principle of separation in a letter to the Danbury Baptist Association in Connecticut: We’re fans of Jefferson’s principle, one of us (Neil Van Leeuwen) being a philosophy professor who grew up in the Christian Reformed Church, and the other (Jacob Eisler) being a law professor who regularly attends synagogue. But Jefferson’s letter makes putting the principle into practice sound simpler than it is – as though governments could be entirely hands-off when it comes to people’s beliefs, or ‘opinions’. That, however, can’t be right: engaging with and shaping political, social and other kinds of beliefs is actually a core function of government. Public education guides what people believe about everything from mathematics to history to astronomy. Governments also shape beliefs for policy reasons. If fire department officials learn that people believe a building is safe when it isn’t, they will try to change what people believe about the building’s safety. Belief is also a category of legal interest. If a psychotherapist, for example, believes a patient is a danger to himself or others, the therapist is obligated by law to report that belief. All this means that Jefferson’s ‘wall of separation’ can’t sensibly prohibit governments from intervening on beliefs of any type whatsoever. Rather, Jefferson – along with advocates of the principle who came after him – must have presupposed that there is something distinctive about religious beliefs that makes it reasonable to give them a different legal status. But what is this distinctive thing? Is it that religious beliefs concern God? No, because Buddhism is a religion with its own beliefs, 
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-psychology-of-separating-church-and-state

* The self-making world
  Aeon | 02 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Long mocked and misunderstood, Lamarck’s radical idea that organisms shape themselves and their world is making a comeback - by Jessica Riskin Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A pod of humpback whales making its way through Alaska’s Inside Passage. Courtesy of Far North Footage/Getty Images The French naturalist Jean-Baptiste Lamarck was 65 when his great treatise on the transformation of life was published. With Philosophie zoologique (1809), translated a century later as Zoological Philosophy , Lamarck intended to establish an entirely new science, one built on the idea that all living beings are constantly transforming themselves, from the tiniest microbes to the most complex mammals, gaining new forms and new abilities. It wasn’t the bodily structures of organisms that determined their ways of life, Lamarck said, but just the opposite: their ways of life in response to their circumstances gave rise to their changing bodily forms. ‘Can there be any consideration in natural history more important,’ he demanded, ‘or deserving of greater attention, than the one I have just set forth?’ (My translation.) But if you’ve heard anything at all about Lamarck, it’s probably how wrong he was about one animal in particular, the giraffe, and how this giraffe fiasco epitomises his wrongness about everything . Lamarck suggested that, by stretching to reach leaves in high tree branches, giraffes slightly elongated their necks, creating tiny changes that were then handed down to their offspring, accumulating over many generations to produce the long necks we see today. Around the turn of the 20th century, Lamarck’s giraffe idea became a laughing stock when biologists roundly rejected the notion that bodily changes undergone in the course of an individual lifetime could be inherited in the next generation. Illustration from Histoire naturelle, générale et particulière (1749-1804) by Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon. Courtesy the Linda Hall Library It wasn’t only his ideas about long-necked mammals that came under attack. Lamarck’s interest in giraffes was just a passing mention in the midst of his voluminous writings, which set out a revolutionary, field-founding theory: organisms, he claimed, transform themselves and, in doing so, they remake the world. That idea has drawn scorn for more than two centuries. Though Lamarck is often overlooked, his science was foundational to modern biology and informed the theory of evolution that Charles Darwin later developed. Darwin followed Lamarck in believing in the agency of living beings to transform themselves and their environment. But most people don’t know about Lamarck’s influence on Darwin b
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-self-making-world-of-jean-baptiste-lamarck
